&lt;!-- PDF Page 14 (Book Page VII) --&gt;

## Preface.

In publishing the Galla Dictionary I am performing a holy duty to the Author of it, my dear, ever-lamented brother Charles. It owes its origin to a three years' unremitted activity, which was unfortunately interrupted by the too early death of my brother, at a time when a long wished-for sphere of action was opened to him, and a future prepared, which, on account of his great talents and intellectual energy, promised to become one of great scientific activity. But although the time granted by Heaven to his endeavours was so short, and but a very small part of what he commenced was completed; yet the results of his studies in the field of African languages, geography, and ethnography, must still be considered a weighty contribution to a nearer acquaintance with that part of the world, still covered with so much obscurity, and one worthy of ranking with the discoveries, made by travels in those countries.

As the present Dictionary is the first of the posthumous works of my brother which has appeared in print, and it may be interesting to those who may have to employ it, as well as to philologists in general, to become acquainted with the manner in which he, far from Africa and in the middle of Germany, proceeded so successfully in the study of the condition and languages of Africa, I will give a short description of the progress

&lt;!-- PDF Page 15 (Book Page VIII) --&gt;
*(— VIII —)*

of my brother, and will endeavour at the same time to make it answer the purpose of a biographical sketch. \*)

My brother Charles Tutschek was born at Bayreuth on the 13. January 1815, and after having passed the Gymnasium he resolved on devoting himself to philology. Certain circumstances compelled him to renounce his favourite study for that of jurisprudence, during the pursuit of which he still, however, occupied himself zealously, as if with a presentiment of his future destination, not only with the living languages, but also with the Sanscrit, Hebrew, and Arabian.

On his arriving in Munich in 1837 to finish his academical studies as a Jurist, he was appointed teacher to Prince Louis of Bavaria, and after the return of His Highness, Duke Maximilian of Bavaria, from his travels in the East, he was entrusted with the education of the four blacks whom the Duke had redeemed from slavery, and brought to Munich. He undertook the unusual duty, — filled with care, on account of the new inter-

> \*) According to the contents of the book, and as far as I remember, according to the original purpose of the Author, a detailed treatise on the Gallas, historical, geographical, and ethnographical, was to be added to the work; but this was unfortunately but begun, and what I can give in regard to this, are mere hints; for not original choice, but sad necessity, has conducted me into the path trodden by my brother, and imposed on me the duty of making what has been furthered by him, tributary to science as much as possible. I think, however, I may express the hope that, if fortunate circumstances concur, I shall be able to tread in the footsteps of my brother, in a more decisive and exclusive manner than has hitherto been the case, and that when I have made some preliminary studies, and can perhaps confer with a young Galla, still living in Germany, in the same manner as my brother, I shall be able to complete much that is now incomplete, and to save for science what now lies useless and uncultivated before me.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 16 (Book Page IX) --&gt;
*(— IX —)*

ruption in his studies on the one side, and the possible failure, from many causes, of the enterprise on the other. Yet the novelty of the thing attracted him, and a scientific desire of learning something interesting, in regard to the home and former circumstances of these strangers, and of thus, perhaps, contributing something to a nearer knowledge of Africa, induced him to commence his work with courage, on the 18. Nov. 1838.

It is easy to be understood how great the difficulties were, at the beginning of a course of instruction in which the teacher was deprived of what is so necessary to produce an effect on the scholar, a means of reciprocal communication, and so much the more when we consider that he had to contend in his first endeavours, with almost insurmountable difficulties in the well-known mistrust in the Africans of all whites, partly inborn and partly produced by circumstances: besides this the boys did not understand what they intended to do with them, and thus many troublesome months passed, during which my brother had to teach daily many hours, laughed at and derided, literally with hands and feet.

At length at the end of January 1839, he gained by a circumstance fortunately taken advantage of, — by kind attention to one of his pupils in a trifling illness, the first great victory, the confidence of the latter; and he soon saw in him as well as in the others, the most obstinate suspicion changed into attachment and heart-felt filial affection. From this moment dates the first fragmentary intelligence of the life of each single one, which above all showed, that whilst the Duke thought he had entrusted to him the education of three Nubians and one Abyssinian, he had given him the representatives of four different tribes, a *Galla*, a *Umale*, a *Darfurian* and a *Denka*. They had been stolen from their homes and had been sold on the Nile as slaves. Necessity had forced them to make themselves mutually understood, to learn the vulgar dialect of the Arabian, though but very imperfectly; as they spoke four languages very different from each other, and even from the known African languages, par-

&lt;!-- PDF Page 17 (Book Page X) --&gt;
*(— X —)*

obtained them, three of them had not been longer than a year from their homes, and, therefore, being sufficiently old and endowed with excellent capacities, they were not only able to give information regarding their languages, but also to form opinions of the circumstances of their countries, which merit a place beside the reports of former travellers in those regions, and can even give explanations, more to be depended on from many causes.

The first results, still gained with great difficulty, of the investigations now commenced, were confined to a confirmation of the travels of Ludolf, Brown, Salt, Seetzen, Burkhardt, Ruppell and Bruce, and of the statements contained in them; to an enrichment of the vocabularies noted down in them; and to a settlement of the relative circumstances of each single individual referred to. The first plans of maps and communication of interesting prayers, were made at this time, as far as their memory and good will could give them; the latter however was often interrupted by a relapse of their former mistrust, and by a taciturnity which lasted whole days together. It required great prudence to go safely to work, as the least inducement often caused them to maintain „that all they had confessed was a fable, and that they knew nothing of their own country, having been taken away when very little children.“ Such relapses, however, became less frequent, nor could they be of long duration, as the conviction that nothing but good was meant them, became firmer and firmer. This was a natural consequence of a nearer acquaintance with our circumstances, and of their progress in their studies, which daily attracted their attention more and more; besides their former accounts were tested carefully by comparisons and repetitions.

The way till now adopted in the mere collection of words was soon given up, and particularly in consequence of the encouragement and advice which my brother received from Professor Charles Ritter of Berlin. My brother had written to this respected

&lt;!-- PDF Page 18 (Book Page XI) --&gt;
*(— XI —)*

scholar, justly considered the first authority in African affairs, regarding his studies, and had the pleasure of receiving, as a mark of the interest he had excited in him, a report of that scanty information, which Professor Ritter had made in the monthly sitting of the geographical society, in May 1839. From this time forward, my brother directed all his attention to continual conversations with his pupils on all the circumstances of their country, although within a very limited horizon; and while his daily increasing enthusiasm succeeded in this, he sought so to nourish and ennoble their desire for home, which still existed, as to make them comprehend, that the communications made to him, united with what they received at his hands, were the safest means of stilling that longing after their fatherland, and also of attaining that nobler aim — of contributing to the welfare of their native country on a foreign soil, whither a hard fate had driven them, and of thus fulfilling a duty imposed on them by a higher hand.

To the method followed from this time forward in this sense, that of allowing them to dictate to him connected and maturely considered representations of subjects, desired by him or originating in themselves, my brother was indebted for those numerous and highly interesting treatises, which afterwards served him, together with the conversation of the persons referred to, as the sources and mines of his philological, geographical, and ethnographical studies. As they are partly written with great solidity and elegance both in regard to style, contents, and arrangement, they may be considered as claiming to represent a sort of literature of the corresponding countries. In order to account for this, notwithstanding the youth of the authorities, it must be remembered, that the art of relating is a talent peculiar to all oriental people, and that those children of nature are, from earliest childhood, intimately acquainted with all the circumstances of their parents, villagers, and tribes, and are, therefore, enabled, with the sharpness of observation peculiar to uncivilized nations, particularly those related to the Semitic ones. When the Duke

&lt;!-- PDF Page 19 (Book Page XII) --&gt;
*(— XII —)*

to attain, at a very early period, to a clear view and opinion of them.

As the treasure gained, increased daily in this manner, the necessity of devoting exclusive attention to the one or the other subject, became more and more apparent. The home and language of the Galla, Akafed'e Dalle (of the tribe of the *Boranna Galla*,) from *Botchi* in *Hambo*, province of *Liban*, who, alas! was not to see his country again, but since 17. Mai 1841 has slept in German ground first attracted my brother's attention. That people which, divided into many tribes, has, since the middle of the 16th century, made itself so historically remarkable, and so dreaded by the Abyssinians on account of its continual inroads and progress towards the North, was excellently represented by the talented Akafad'e. My brother, too, without taking into consideration the political circumstances, (which, however, threw a considerable weight into the scale,) was particularly attracted by the simplicity, euphony, and grammatical formation of the Galla language which became evident after but a very short study. He directed, therefore, without neglecting the others, his chief attention to that, and in the short period of a year and a half, he succeeded so far as to speak with tolerable fluency, and to be able to accept the first invitation from the Royal Academy of Munich, to communicate to them the results of his studies. In the sitting of the philosophical class on the 2. January 1841, he read a report of the commencement and progress of his investigations, and the sketch of a grammar of the Galla language; at the same time he laid before the class a tolerably complete Dictionary of the Galla Language, and many dictations, prayers, and songs, with commentaries regarding the language and circumstances. A treatise he had received a short time before on the Galla Language in *Limu* (Extrait du bulletin de la société de geographie Aout 1839), read by Mr. Jomard, Librarian, in the Academy in Paris, was the first written proof of the correctness of his own results, for he found in it but well known facts in the geographical parts, as also in

&lt;!-- PDF Page 20 (Book Page XIII) --&gt;
*(— XIII —)*

the few specimens of language. The prayers are the same written down by his Akafad'e, excepting a difference in the orthography, which naturally produces a corresponding difference in the translation. What is there said of manners and customs, agrees, with few exceptions, with his observations. But what was of more consequence than this, was the speaking proof which good fortune brought him, in the person of a second Galla. Otshu Aga from *Urgeza* in *Sibu* was placed, for two months, at the disposition of my brother by his deliverer from slavery, Mr. Pell, on his passing through Munich. By him not only all he had gained and learned, was confirmed; but materials were also given for dialectical investigations; and the number of dictations, prayers, and songs, with geographical data etc. were increased by many interesting additions. Between Aga and Akafed'e, who, sadly revelling in the remembrance of their lost country, soon concluded a bond of friendship, my brother caused a Galla correspondence. It consisted of 14 letters, which, besides their philological worth, form a good scale of the dispositions of mind and soul of the correspondents. Through Aga my brother also got acquainted with the supposed Abyssinian girl of Prince Pückler Muscau, Bilile (who died 27. Oct. 1840). She was from the Galla-country *Guma*, and had tought Aga many of her songs which are also among the number of those noted down by my brother. Lastly a third Galla, Aman Gonda from *Hibi* in *Goma*, brought to Europe by his Highness Duke Paul of Wirtemberg, closed the number of his authorithies. The latter he visited twice at the invitation of this scientific prince, and staid a considerable time with him. My brother laid great value on the communications of Aman, although he is the youngest of all three, because he, being the son of an *Aba Fuño* (lit. master of the cord, a sort of magistrate in the service of the *Zoreza* or prince), had enjoyed a better education than the other two, and was, therefore, better acquainted with the circumstances of his country, and spoke his language more correctly.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 21 (Book Page XIV) --&gt;
*(— XIV —)*

As these three individuals passed in a certain order before my brother's enquiring eye, the comparisons and additions naturally produced considerable variations and enrichments of his former labours. Thus the Dictionary, which, when laid before the Academy, was very weak in regard to orthography and etymology, has been put into the present improved form, built on a solid foundation, and brought in unison with the principles exhibited in the Grammar. \*)

Besides this more exclusive occupation with his Galla, the investigation of the circumstances of the home and language of a second of his pupils continued, who raised his interest in an equal, if not a still higher degree. It was Djalo Djondan Aré of the people of the *Yumales*, who live below *Kordofan* under the 12° and 13° N. L. and the 46° and 47° E. L. under the sovereignty of the King of *Takle* (*Takele*, *Tokole*). He was born at *Deling'n* in *Tumale-Tokoken*, and is the nephew of the hereditary prince (king) *Wofter Mat*, who was still reigning there at his departure. His language represents in regard to

> \*) The second part of the Dictionary, English-Galla, and if it should be considered advantageous, the third part also: German-Galla, I shall publish as soon after the first as possible. The above named grammar has, unfortunately, not received the same elaboration in all its parts, as in the verbs, numbers, and pronouns; and the samples of language which my brother intended to add as an appendix to his grammar, are not properly arranged for this purpose. This is very much to be regretted, particularly in regard to the syntax of the Galla language, as my brother had collected so many materials for it, and always described the philological charms of it, as so great and enticing. I shall, however, give the grammar in its imperfect form to the public, as my brother has left it, but shall add some samples of language, as I think, to advantage, together with the plans of maps.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 22 (Book Page XV) --&gt;
*(— XV —)*

sound, character, and internal formation, the contrary of the Galla language; for, while the latter sounds soft and melodious, not unlike the Italian language, and has like this, with a great preponderance of vowels a proportional simplicity of form and expression, the language of the *Umale* sounds hard and even rugged; and offers, on account of the great number of its consonants, great difficulties of pronunciation, even to our German organs, accustomed to hard sounds. The forms are also so very various and peculiar, and the number of words and ideas in this language, is so superior to that in the Galla, that if my brother had lived, he would have required a much longer time and much more labour to penetrate into it, than he had required for the Galla language, which he had learnt in so comparatively short a time. But exactly this richness of the language, and still more the high degree of civilization of the *Yumale*, (if I may use this expression of a people of central Africa,) upon which the communications of the highly gifted Djalo lead us to conclude, encouraged my brother to do the same with this as the Galla; for however great the difficulties may be, which the learning and comprehension of this language offer; yet the lexicographical and grammatical elaboration of it, would be so advantageous to the philologer in general, and the treasures which the knowledge of it would discover, are so great, that any one would be sufficiently rewarded for his trouble. Djalo's dictations \*) particularly, deserve the praises that have been bestowed above on their sterling worth, as to form and contents; and to the above must be added that they all bear the impression of mature judgment and critical truth,

> \*) I have three volumes of them in my possession, in which every possible relation of that interesting people is treated on, partly in fragments and partly at large. The communications are divided into such as are the reproduction of the excellent memory of Djalo, and such as are his own productions as free compositions.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 23 (Book Page XVI) --&gt;
*(— XVI —)*

a union of advantages which will once make them appear as an essential enrichment of science, when they have been made known to the public, after having been sifted and commented upon. From this description, certainly not an exaggerated one, it will be perceived, that I have inherited the enthusiasm of my brother for these interesting studies, and I seize here the opportunity of mentioning, that I am firmly resolved, as far as it lies in my weak power, to follow the path entered by my brother, also in this, and to pay, partly at least, what he has remained indebted to science. The beginning is made, for Djalo is living at Munich, and assisted by his lively endeavours to co-operate in the good work, I have already been introduced by daily conferences with him, into the porch of the great building to be examined, and whether I shall arrive at a satisfactory goal will be very soon discovered.

The third pupil of my brother is Mussalam Motekutu from *Methem* in *Darfur*, about 7 days' journey to the south of *Kobbe*, the capital of the country. His information is also not of inconsiderable value, and his dictations, too, well merit elaboration; for although they bear a more childish stamp upon them both in style and contents, and the reminiscences of Mussalam do not seem to be so comprehensive as those of the before named authorities of my brother; yet the recommencement of inquiries regarding his home and language, now that he is grown more mature, will not only afford us various ethnographical notices, but will also render it possible to complete a very rich vocabulary together with the draught of a grammar of his language. Mussalam still speaks it fluently, and as far as my trifling insight into its construction and character allow me to form a judgment, it does not offer such difficulties, by far, to learning it, as that of the *Umale* does.

Lastly, of the fourth pupil Awan, I will let my brother speak himself. In the above mentioned lecture at the Academy, he said among other things: „In this boy, the youngest of all, (he may be about 12 years old,) I have observed something

&lt;!-- PDF Page 24 (Book Page XVII) --&gt;
*(— XVII —)*

psycologically astonishing, which deserves to be made the subject of more particular consideration. During the whole year namely, that I was progressing so gaily with the others, he always answered very sadly that he did not know any thing of his home, having been too young when he left it. In the beginning I thought it was the old mistrust that expressed itself in him; but having found him candid in every thing else, I took his assertion for truth, and left him unobserved for a considerable time; particularly as the other sources flowed so richly. This involuntary neglect on my part, united with the wantonness towards the weaker, so often to be observed among boys, was near producing bad consequences. The boy became still, morose, — and at last I found him crying many days together, without being able to discover the cause. When alone with me, however, he told me that he was home-sick and that he longed to see his relations; he said too that he remembered the name of his father, mother, and brother, but that I would not believe him. Repenting of my former conduct I assured the weeping boy of my perfect confidence, and wrote the names down; then I pressed him further, and asked him if he did not remember any word of his language, and, after long consideration, he actually called to mind two: *kur*, mountain, and *kir*, river. To my great joy I found them both in Rüppel's Vocabulary under the title of *Dar Denka*. After having, on the following days, by continual persuasion on my part, and meditation on his, succeeded in remembering 6 or 8 words which agreed with those in Rüppels Vocabulary, I mentioned *Denkawi*, the name of the people, which he recognised immediately as the name of his people. It was delightful to go through the above mentioned Vocabulary with him and to observe the effect of the well known sounds upon the boy; and it was also moving to see the joy of this three black friends, whom I found, a quarter of an hour after they had learnt the news, weeping round his neck, full of joy at his becoming, as it were, of equal birth to them, by finding his native country. From that moment there also began for him quite a different life. By continual meditation
*(Sig. 2)*

&lt;!-- PDF Page 25 (Book Page XVIII) --&gt;
*(— XVIII —)*

upon the past, he succeeded in writing a vocabulary of about 500 words, and I have not given up the hope of his recalling something connected.“ The latter hope was not fulfilled; Awan's contribution was limited to an increased vocabulary.

It will be seen by the foregoing that the task which my brother had imposed upon himself, was a great and manifold one, and that the acknowledgments which the partial solution of it, and his endeavours in general, called forth from the Munich learned world, and from foreign literati, and which acted so encouragingly upon him, were not undeserved. But what acted most beneficially on the furtherance and success of his work, particularly at the time when his relation to his pupils was dissolved, was the active participation testified by the Geographical Society and the African Civilisation Society in London in repeated letters, and material assistance rendered him, through the helping hand of Sir Thomas Dyke Acland, the Vicepresident of the latter Society. Sir Thomas Acland not only sent all the books referring to his labours, which had appeared in later times, but also a voluntary pecuniary support, thus alone enabling my brother to pursue his studies, which otherwise might have met with a highly disadvantageous interruption. I consider it my duty to make this public acknowledgement so much the more, as Sir Thomas Acland made this sacrifice purely out of interest in the cause of science. This participation and the prospect united with it, of being employed in a scientific mission or expedition proceeding from England to Africa, and finally the interest which a German prince took in his studies, supported his wavering constitution for a considerable time. When the present Dictionary was finished, it was laid before His Royal Highness the Crown Prince of Bavaria, and my brother then expressed a wish of pursuing his studies in Egypt in a similar manner, by the redemption and scientific investigation of black men. The Crown Prince, who is ever delighted to give assistance to every truly scientific endeavour, being struck with the sterling merit of the work, immediately agreed to the proposal of my

&lt;!-- PDF Page 26 (Book Page XIX) --&gt;
*(— XIX —)*

brother; and after having had an estimate drawn out, of the expences of such a journey to Egypt, he promised, three weeks, afterwards, the sum necessary for a residence of two years in Cairo. But unfortunately this appointment was, as it were, but a view of the promised land, for notwithstanding the endeavours of the first physicians in Munich, and the use of the baths at Ems, purchased with great sacrifices, his corporeal powers diminished more and more, and after his return to his native town, he fell a victim to the malady which had been undermining his constitution for the last year, in the house of his parents on the 6. of September 1843. Thus ended, much too early, a life which had set itself the great aim of actively co-operating in the civilization of Africa, this endeavour worthy of our century, and thus of adding his mite to the realization of the idea of European cultivation and civilization, becoming the common property of all people. Although death prevented him from relating to the world, with his own mouth, what he had heard and seen in his mental wanderings, and from proving to them that it is well worth the trouble to feel some interest for those people; yet by the written words he has left behind him, it is evident that the harsh judgment which has been pronounced by so many, on the moral and mental state of all the central African nations, and which keeps so many from directing their benevolence, and, at the same time, their activity to them, deserves a mitigation, to produce which was a second holy aim of my brother. The means for this were sufficiently laid down in the unconstrained narratives of his pupils, who were justly considered by him as a sufficient authority to be placed beside the African travellers, confirming and correcting them. May his wish and desire be fulfilled, that the way so seldom trodden till now, and pursued almost exclusively by the English, namely, that of acting upon Africans by means of Africans, may no longer remain so unfrequented; may his example find imitators among the Germans; and may the beginning which he has made, no longer remain a beginning, but be the point of departure, the first link
*(Sig. 2\*)*

&lt;!-- PDF Page 27 (Book Page XX) --&gt;
*(— XX —)*

of a chain of similar endeavours, by the joint operations of which we may share the renown which the wonderful activity of England in Africa, is so busied in gaining, to so great an extent.



The following remarks are intended to facilitate the employment of the present Dictionary, and the comprehension of the geographical and ethnographical circumstances of the people, whose language is partly represented here.

The Gallas who, as has been already observed, dwell in numerous tribes \*) round the south of Abyssinia, have, according, to my brother's investigations, most probably received their name from their own language, and not from the Abyssinians as most authors (Tellez, Ludolf, and all later writers with the exception of Bruce) suppose. He derives it, according to his view, from the verb *gala*, to go home, to seek one's home, and is to be brought in connection with the historical fact, that the Gallas, driven by some cause or other from their homes in 1735, rushed in torrents towards Abyssinia, and made this country their home, — a view, in favour of which, besides other reasons, the circumstance speaks, of their calling themselves by this name. This great assemblage of tribes whose extent towards central Africa is not yet known, may have formerly led an erratic pastoral life, but this is no longer the case. The Gallas live in the districts which my brother became more nearly acquainted with, in villages, which as they consist of single farms, surrounded by a great extent of land, are probably very straggling in part.

The chief occupations of the Gallas are agriculture and cattle breeding. The former is carried on so extensively in the environs of the villages, that, from a scarcity of fodder for their numerous herds, very distant pastures must often be sought for, which causes continual disputes with the neighbouring tribes. In the villages artisans even, particularly weavers, tanners, potters, leather-cutters, metal-workers etc. etc., provide them with the necessaries of life and warlike stores.

> \*) Krapf (Journals of the Rev. Mess. Isenberg and Krapf etc. Lond. 1843 and, An imperfect outline of the elements of the Galla-Language by the Rev. I. L. Krapf. Lond. 1840) mentions more than 60 by name.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 28 (Book Page XXI) --&gt;
*(— XXI —)*

The form of government is in the free tribes, i. e. those that are not subject or tributary to any neighbouring power (Abyssinias etc.) monarchical-despotical, and the dignity of king (*moti*) devolves by inheritance to the male descendants, (in some few of the tribes also to the female,) or the *moti* is changed by election, which is renewed after a certain number of years. Under the *moti* stand the *zoreza* (princes) from among whom the heads of places (*aba ganda*) are chosen, while the other mighty men of the tribe are only *aba lafa*, a condition which answers that of our noble lords of manors or landed proprietors.

Their religion is a monotheism obscured by many superstitions. The Gallas adore a supreme being, *Wacᵃ* (originally heaven), who is almighty, omniscient, all-good, all-wise, in short, possessing all the qualities which we Christians attribute to our God. The worship consists in prayers and sacrifices, (the adoration of a tree is most decidedly impugned by my brother,) and penetrates deep into all the circumstances of life. If this natural religion has not been brought into some system, which is very probable, it is evident from these communications, that the moral views connected with it, exercise a very beneficial and profound effect on the manner of thinking and acting of its confessors, — a circumstance which might lead us to suppose, that we should find here a soil, well fitted for the reception and propagation of christian doctrines.

Of the manners and customs I will but mention a few. Polygamy is permitted, and very frequent. The number of wives is according to the property of the man. In this respect the king enjoys the privilege of taking, besides his lawful wives (*qena*), girls out of any families, and without asking permission, making them his concubines (*zadjeda*), which they have no objection to, it being considered a great honour. If a common man wish to marry, (which every youth does as soon as he is arrived at the age of puberty \*), he goes to the father of the girl he has chosen, and demands her, at the same time informing him how much property he has (in oxen, horses, sheep

> \*) Akafed'e was much surprised at seeing so many grown up men in Europe, who would not marry; he would have taken a wife, he said, had he remained at home, for it is so pleasant to have a son to inherit the love, and after their death the property of the parents.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 29 (Book Page XXII) --&gt;
*(— XXII —)*

etc. If there are many wooers, that one takes her home, to whom the girl gives a gold ring. On a certain day in presence of the relations and friends of both parties the marriage is then concluded according to law, with prayers and religious ceremonies. The woman receives her portion after her first son is born; if she bears a daughter she receives nothing or little, and this circumstance generally causes separation. As the female sex holds a very inferior rank, this has no difficulty, and if the husband pleases, he can drive her out of his house, for other causes than that above-mentioned. In such cases, or if even the woman herself desires the separation, or runs away, the proposition: Partus sequitur ventrem, is not valid, but the husband keeps the children, for it would be considered a crime for him to desert them. — Hospitality and respect for age are virtues generally practised. — Smoking is very common among the Gallas, particularly among the soldiers (which they are all actually). It seems that they mix intoxicating herbs with their tobacco, for Akafed'e relates of very strong smokers having sometimes died in a few days with violent coughing. — The beard is shorn of the common people, but by the princes and priests it is worn long. — Inviolability of oaths. — Auspices and Sooth-saying. — Games, Dances, accompanied by metrical and rhymed songs etc. etc.

Trade is confined to barter, in which the usual conventional coins used are pieces of rock-salt (*amole*) and its particles.

What will be of great advantage in our intercourse with this people, is the Galla tribes all having the same language, excepting a few dialectical variations and a greater or less intermixture of foreign words.

Whether the Gallas have a method of writing and what method it is has not been decided to a certainty; for the letter supposed to be written in Galla-characters, which Mr. d'Abbadie sent to Paris, is not deciphered; yet this document causes us to presume the existence of a mode of writing. If one is extant it must be composed of entirely peculiar letters or syllables, as no alphabet or syllabarium can express, with exactness, all the sounds met with in the Galla-Language. \*) My brother was, therefore, frequently obliged to have

> \*) What Mr. Krapf says on this subject in his Imperfect outline, namely, „that no sound is found in the Galla-Language that cannot be expressed with English letters, even better than with Ethiopian“, is very doubtful.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 30 (Book Page XXIII) --&gt;
*(— XXIII —)*

recourse, in fixing the orthography, to certain signs, which added to our letters, endeavour to point out the peculiar character of the different Galla sounds. The number of letters adopted by him at the last revisal of his Dictionary amounts to 30; but, in the later investigations of this language, it will not stop here, for many sounds became evident to the sharpened ear of the observer, which escaped him at first, and which must be added to the alphabet adopted by him, in case this should be retained. To these belong, for example an entirely unaspirated T-sound, lying between the *t* and *d*; probably also a *p*, if the cases here referred to, do not indicate a gemination of the *b*; further, a peculiar *l* almost entirely the dead Polish *ł*; then a nasal sound, perhaps only belonging to the idiom of *Goma* which in the mouth of both the other authorities answers an *n* before a *z*, for instance *qile˜za*, instead of *qilenza*; *ma˜gaga* instead of *mangaga* etc.

The single letters have not been arranged in the usual manner, but in families, which order is observed throughout and will be excused on account of the inadequacy of the existing alphabets, although it may cause at first some inconvenience in looking out for them. The table of them is as follows:

### Table of the letters. \*)

| Col. 1 | Col. 2 | Col. 3 | Col. 4 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1. *a*, *á*, *ã*, *ȧ* | 9. *tch* | 17. *f* | 25. *ñ* |
| 2. *e*, *ê* | 10. *tsh* | 18. *b* | 26. *z* |
| 3. *i* | 11. *dj* | 19. *y* | 27. *ç* |
| 4. *o* | 12. *dy* | 20. *w* | 28. *z'* |
| 5. *u* | 13. *t* | 21. *r* | 29. *h* |
| 6. *k* | 14. *t'* | 22. *l* | 30. *ʿ* |
| 7. *c* resp. *q* | 15. *d* | 23. *m* | |
| 8. *g* | 16. *d'* | 24. *n* | |

> \*) This table and the following Remarks are taken out of the Grammar.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 31 (Book Page XXIV) --&gt;
*(— XXIV —)*

### Remarks on the Alphabet.

#### I. Vowels.

*a* sounds everywhere like the clear English a in „father.“ It can be long or short. Long either by nature or from the contraction of
- *aa*, for example: *bá*, I go out, contracted from *baa*;
- *awa*, e. g. *lakã*, I count, contr. from *lakawa*;
- *aya*, e. g. *tȧ*, I sit, contracted from *taya*.

At the end of words *a* is very often almost quite mute or spoken so short that it seems to be only a breath and aiming at, as it were, the sound which it denotes, as in the Tumale language. I have marked this vowel by writing it over the line in this manner: *Wacᵃ*, heaven; *zogitᵃ*, salt. When the word takes an addition from declension, conjugation or contraction, the full sound appears again, which proves that it becomes mute only from haste in speaking. The same is the case with all other vowels, wherefore I would have it remarked here directly. Examples are: *Akafed'ᵉ*, a name; *torbanⁱ*, seven, *tokᵒ*, one, *Sibᵘ*, the name a Galla-country.

*e* has two different sounds: 1. like a in fate; for example: *beka*, I know; *teza*, thou sittest; — 2. e in met, or like the German ae in: „Saege, Väter.“ I have marked it thus: *mêka*, how much? — *rêfa*, a dead body.

*i* sounds like i in pin; *o* like o in no; *u* like oo. All these vowels are likewise either long or short.

#### II. Diphthongs.

The Galla is devoid of real diphthongs, that is to say, of such which already lie in the roots of the words themselves, the particle „*wau*“, „no“, must be the only exception, but even this word would perhaps be spelt better with *w* than with *u*. All the other combinations of vowels are merely formed by declension or conjugation. In the examples: *bai* (Imperative mood of *baa* I go out); *tau*, to sit; *himboin*, do not weep; *himbuin*, do not fall etc. the *i* and *u* are always characteristic of a certain mood or tense, but never an element of a natural diphthong.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 32 (Book Page XXV) --&gt;
*(— XXV —)*

#### III. Consonants.

*k* is always the hard palatial sound as in key, kill.

*c* (before *e* and *i*; — *q*) is a softer sound produced by pressing the tongue against the middle of the palate and pushing or smacking with it, but without an aspiration, whilst the afore-said sound proceeds with a certain aspiration deeper from the hindpart of the mouth. This sound requires great accuracy, because by its being confounded with *k* or *g*, mistakes are unavoidable; thus *caca* is soot; *gaga* wax; *kaka* the root of *kakad'a*, I swear, take an oath.

*g* has every-where the hard sound of the English g in go, gain, nor is it ever melted with *n* to that nasal sound ng, so frequent in the European languages. The latter sound is entirely wanting in Galla and wherever *ng* occours the two consonants must be spoken separately, for example: *dan-ga*, not *danga*, nourishment. Before *m* *g* appears assimilated to it in the dialect of Sibu; viz. Aga said instead of *dagma* — *damma*, honey.

*ch* is employed only in a few cases as a euphonical softening of *k* before *n* and *t* in the flexion of verbs, for example: *ani beka*, I know; *ati bechta*, thou knowest; *nu bechna*, we know; its pronunciation is then the same, with that of the German ch in „dich, mich“.

The three sounds *tch*, *dj*, *dy* which from their nature lie between the *K*- and *T*-sounds, have been adapted to the English pronunciation; *tch* being the harsh sound tch in „fetch“; *dj* as if it were dsh, but softer; and *dy* still softer than this. But besides these three letters I have availed myself also of *dz'* and *d'z'*, in order to preserve the root in verbal forms, because by changing them into *tch* or *dj* it would often become impossible to distinguish the latter, for example: the III. form of *d'êd'a*, eat (said of cattle), I have written *d'êd'z'iza* (I feed), not *d'êtchiza* or *d'êdjiza*, on purpose to show, that the final consonant of the root is *d'*, not *t*, *d* nor *t'*, which is of importance to be known in the conjugation.

*t*, *t'*, *d*, *d'*. A right and accurate pronunciation and writing of the *T*-sounds, offer greater difficulties than all the other letters in Galla and the utmost precaution ought to be observed not to confound them with each other, because almost always the distinguishing of the words depends on it. I never in any other language found such an exact and sharp distinction in this class of consonants, even when spoken as swiftly as possible, as in the mouth of the Gallas. And that is

&lt;!-- PDF Page 33 (Book Page XXVI) --&gt;
*(— XXVI —)*

the reason, why in the inflection of verbs, when another *t* is joined to a root ending with a *T*-sound, and an assimilation of both seems to be natural or even requisite, both are exhibited, in order to make the root discernible, in this manner:
*fidta*, thou bringest, from *ani fida*; *ñad'ta*, thou eatest, from *ani ñad'a*; *fit'ta*, thou endest from *ani fit'a*.

The single *T*-sounds are as follows:
1) *t*, our hard and the German aspirated t and th as in „Tanne, Thau.“
2) *t'*, a *T*-sound peculiar to this language and a very hard one, in pronouncing which the tongue presses itself closely against the upper fore-teeth, so that after the *t'* almost a soft s becomes audible, and the z that is wanting seems to be supplied. Examples: *t'ut'a*, I smoke; *t'ife*, blue. Perhaps *t'* has its analogy in the Amharic classe of letters represented by the *T'ait*, *Tsh'aït* and *Ts'adai*.

   Before *n* the three *T*-sounds *d*, *t* and *d'* are assimilated without causing a doubling of the *n*, the *t'* howewer rests unchanged; for example: *fud'a*, I take, plur. *funa*, we take, but *fit'a*, I finish, plur. *fit'na*, we finish.
3) *d* is our soft d in day, load, maid; for example, *diña*, enemy; *eda*, because.
4) *d'*, a singular sound, scarcely to be expressed with our organs. It is very soft and formed by a mild push of the tongue from the hindpart of the palate, so that between the *d'* and the following vowel another consonant seems to be intercalated, similar to the Semitic Ayin. It forms thus to a certain degree the bridge from the *T*-sounds to the gutturals and before the ear is well accustomed to this particular idiom, it is usually confounded with *g*. Examples are: *d'ufa*, I come; *mid'an*, grain.

Of how great importance the precise distinction between the before mentioned *T*-sounds is, may be seen in the following words
- *d'uta*. 2. pers. pres. of *dua*, I die,
- *duda*. adj. deaf,
- *d'ud'ã*. v. I devour,
- *tuta*. subst. crowd, multitude,
- *t'ut'a*. v. I smoke,

&lt;!-- PDF Page 34 (Book Page XXVII) --&gt;
*(— XXVII —)*

these five words being of the same signification, if not distinguished by a right pronunciation of the respective *T*-sounds.

*f* has always the sharp sound of f in fat, fall; a softer sound for this consonant being wanting.

*b* in the beginning of words like our b in bind, bow; in the middle, particularly before vowels almost like v, for example: *d'abe*, he did not find, spell: *d'ave*. Before *n* it sounds almost like *m*; Ex. *tchabnani* (partic. pret. of *tchaba*, I break) spell *tchamnani*. But here also a real assimilation of *b* with the related liquid *m* might be supposed.

*y* sounds as in English in the beginning as well as in the middle of words, but being a consonant it never forms a diphthong; which may seen particularly in the primitive form of the verbs, which I regard as representing the 2nd conjugation, and still more in their II. form of roots; as *kȧ*, contracted from *kaya*, II. f. *kayad'a*; where the *y* must be regarded not as forming the diphtong *ay* with the vowel *a*, but beginning the next syllable, in this manner: *ka-ya*, — *ka-ya-d'a*.

*w* must be pronounced in the beginning and in the middle of words like the english w, so that almost a diphthong seems to be heard; for *rawad'a*, I finish, sounds almost like *rauwad'a*.

*r* like our r.

*l* has its usual sound; when it stands before *n*, the *n* is assimilated with it and the *l* appears doubled, f. e. *ilala*, I see; 1. pers. plur. pres. *ilalla*, instead of *ilalna*, we see.

*m* is the same as in other languages.

*n* likewise. Before *l*, *m*, *n*, *r* it is assimilated and forms *ll*, *mm*, *nn*, *rr*. Before *b* it is changed into *m* and before *w* it disappears quite. Respecting *ṅ*, marked thus: *ṅ*, the negative *n*, as I call it, see this letter in the Dictionary pag. 176.

*ñ* is a sound which occupies tongue, nose and palate at once and so singularly that I could by no means hit the right pronunciation; it is similar to the Italian gn in legno or to the Spanish ñ, but without any admixture of i, which is more or less the case in those sounds. Whether it has any sound analogous in the Amharic Gnahas or not, I do not know; I doubt it however, because — as it is by Ludolf simply expressed with the Italian gn — the pronunciation of Gnahas ought to be much easier than this sound, so opposed to our organ.

*z* marks the soft

*ç* the sharp s.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 35 (Book Page XXVIII) --&gt;
*(— XXVIII —)*

*z'* or *z'* as I have remarked above expresses the English sh.

*h* is found only in the beginning of words and is the simple aspiration as in our languages.

*ʿ* the same consonant as the Semitic Ayin, wherefore I have chosen its character. It occurs in the middle and at the end of words and causes in the first case a singular disjunction of the word by a certain kind of pause as in *harʿa*, to-day; in the other a breathless shortness of the last vowel as in *qileʿ*, deep; *reeʿ*, goat.

The indisputable propensity of the Galla-language to vowels, which is so great that not one word ends with a consonant, — then, that no word begins with two consonants and that, wherever by inflection in declension or conjugation, three consonants would meet together, the existing hardness is avoided by intercalating a vowel or displacing the consonants themselves; finally that the harder consonants *t*, *t'*, *k*, *tch* etc. are proportionally rare, while the softer ones *d*, *d'*, *dj*, *b*, *g* etc. preponderate, make the language, particularly in the mouth of a native Galla, very soft, and especially qualifies it for versification. This property also appears to be the reason why, although the Galla poetry be otherwise so poor, rhyme is nevertheless so cultivated that rhymes in the middle as well as at the ends of lines frequently occur.

### Formal remarks.

All verbs are mentioned in the 1st Pers. Sing. Pres. and the signification is added in the Infinitive for the sake of brevity.

The numbers 1, 2, 3 standing after the Galla verb, denote the conjugation according to which it is to be inflected, which is determined by the 2nd Pers. Sing. Pres.; thus, for example, 1st Conj. *adema*, I go; *ademta*, thou goest. — 2nd Conj. *tshiza*, I lie; *tschifta*, thou liest. — 3rd Conj. *tȧ*, I stand; *teza* thou standest. (The first signification of every word has been left without the mark „1.“ to avoid any mistakes in regard to the conjugation.)

The enclosed syllables (*-aa*, *-awa*, *-aya*) have been added to the contracted ones in *á*, *ã* and *ȧ*, to show their original form.

The Roman numbers I. II. III. to VIII. which are ranged under the roots point out the newly arisen verbs called by my brother „forms“. They are produced by additional syllables which modify the signification of the root according to certain fixed rules, and answer to the different conjugations in the Semitic languages. As an example to

&lt;!-- PDF Page 36 (Book Page XXIX) --&gt;
*(— XXIX —)*

render this intelligible the verb *bá* with the 8 forms in use complete, will be found below:
- **I.** *bá*, (contr. from *baa*) the root, to go out of something, to go out.
- **II.** *bad'a*, to go out for one's self, to go out to one's own advantage or disadvantage, thence: 2. to lose one's self, to be lost.
- **III.** *baza*, to cause one to go out, to make one go out; to drive away; to pay.
- **IV.** *bafad'a*, to make one go out for one's self; to drive out for one's self; to pay for one's self.
- **V.** *baziza*, to cause that one makes one go out, or sends out; drives out, pays, pardons.
- **VI.** *(printed IV.)* *bazifad'a*, to have one driven out for one's self, for one's advantage; to have one paid, to have one pardoned for one's self, for one's own advantage.
- **VII.** *baziziza*, to cause that one has one sent out, paid, pardoned etc.
- **VIII.** *bazizifad'a*, the same, but with the medial shade.

All the substantives are given in the accusative and the letters or syllables annexed: *n.* *i.* *ni.* *fni.* *fti* etc. indicate the form of the nominative. Namely, one peculiarity of the Galla-Language is, that the accusative is always the simplest form, although not always the pure root, and my brother's authorities always answered, when a substantive was required, with an accusative.

As the accent is of great consequence it has been added to every word, and is expressed by the letters ox. px. and ppx. (oxyton, paroxyton, proparoxyton). The only exceptions are the contracted verbs, which are not provided with the accent in any part of the manuscript of my brother, probably because it is a general rule, that they are all pronounced as oxytons in the contracted form: as, *bobaa*. px. contracted: *bobá*. ox. — But I cannot answer for the infallibility of this hypothesis, because I have not been able to find any remark upon the subject anywhere, but can only rely in this respect upon my memory which has retained the above mentioned *bobá* as an oxyton, having heard it pronounced as such by the mouth of my brother.

The numerous references to the Grammar shortly to be published were raised to actual value during the printing of the Dictionary, the arrangement according to numbers, not having been made by my

&lt;!-- PDF Page 37 (Book Page XXX) --&gt;
*(— XXX —)*

brother's but my hand; many of the references were therefore obliged to be struck out of the manuscript, partly because I could not find the corresponding rules and statements in the Grammar, partly because many are in fact not there, many parts of the Grammar, as has been observed above, being incomplete, and some (the syntax for instance) failing entirely.

### Abbreviations.

**Ag.** Aga. — **Ak.** or **Akaf.** Akafed'e. — **Am.** Aman. (the 3 Galla authorities of my brother, see above Page XII. and XIII.) — **Aeth.** Aethiopian. — **Amh.** Amharic. — **Ar.** Arabic. — **Bot.** Botany. — **H. P.** Duke Paul William of Wirtemberg, who during the residence of my brother at Mergentheim had the kindness to explain many plants to my brother botanically. — **esp.** especially. — **f.** Form. — **id.** idem. — **jur.** juridical. — **inv.** invariable, of substantives, whose form of the nominative is equal to that of the accusative case. — **Magm.** *Magmaga*, Proverb. — **Med.** Medium, middle signification. — **nat. sound**, a word formed after the natural sound. — **recipr.** reciprocal. — **refl.** reflective. — **Wetu**, Song.

In here taking leave of the reader, I again express my most heart-felt gratitude to Sir Thomas Dyke Acland and other noble patrons in London, for the interest they have manifested in the labours of my brother, and for their endeavours to render the publication of this Dictionary possible by subscription; as also to my Munich friends and patrons for their assistance in word and deed, in the performance of this task. To the public I repeat the devout wish, that the endeavours of my dear brother, of which this Dictionary will bear testimony to greater circles, may find manifold imitations; and that this work even in its imperfect form, may serve the great purpose to which he devoted his useful life, too soon cut short for me, his friends, and science.

**Munich Sept. 1844.**

***The Editor.***

&lt;!-- PDF Page 38 (Book Page XXXI) --&gt;

#### SEINER KÖNIGLICHEN HOHEIT

# MAXIMILIAN

#### KRONPRINZEN VON BAYERN

#### DEM HOHEN GÖNNER DES VERFASSERS DIESES LEXICONS

##### WIDMET DASSELBE

#### IN TIEFSTER EHRFURCHT UND DANKBARKEIT

***DER HERAUSGEBER.***

&lt;!-- PDF Page 39 (Book Page XXXII): Blank --&gt;

&lt;!-- PDF Page 40 (Book Page XXXIII) --&gt;

## Vorrede.

Indem ich das Galla-Lexicon hiemit der Oeffentlichkeit übergebe, trage ich eine heilige Schuld an dem Verfasser desselben, meinen theuren, unvergesslichen Bruder Karl ab. Es verdankt sein Entstehen einer dreijährigen angestrengten Thätigkeit, welche aber leider durch den viel zu frühen Tod meines Bruders gerade zu einer Zeit gebrochen wurde, wo sich ihm neuer, lang ersehnter Wirkungskreis eröffnet und eine Zukunft vorbereitet hatte, die bei seinen vortrefflichen Anlagen und seiner geistigen Energie mit Recht als eine wissenschaftlich thatenreiche vorausgesetzt werden durfte. So kurz jedoch auch die Zeit war, die der Himmel seinem Streben gegönnt, und obwohl das von ihm Begonnene nur zum kleinsten Theile zur Vollendung gedieh, so werden immerhin die Resultate seiner Studien auf dem Felde africanischer Linguistik, Geographie und Ethnographie als wichtige Beiträge zur näheren Kenntniss jenes noch mit so vielseitigem Dunkel bedeckten Welttheils betrachtet werden müssen und sich würdig an das durch Reisen in die entsprechenden Länder Gewonnene anreihen.

Da gegenwärtiges Lexicon das erste Werk ist, welches aus dem Nachlasse meines Bruders im Druck erscheint und es sowohl denen, die in den Fall kommen, dasselbe zu benützen als auch überhaupt allen Freunden der Sprachforschung in-
*(Sig. 3)*

&lt;!-- PDF Page 41 (Book Page XXXIV) --&gt;
*(— XXXIV —)*

teressant sein mag, den Weg kennen zu lernen, auf welchem ferne von Africa, inmitten Deutschlands das Studium africanischer Zustände und Sprachen mit so schönem Erfolge gepflegt wurde, so werde ich in Folgendem kurz den Bildungsgang meines Bruders darzustellen und damit zugleich den Zweck, eine biographische Skizze zu geben, zu erreichen suchen. \*)

Mein Bruder, Karl Tutschek, war geboren zu Bayreuth am 13. Januar 1815 und hatte sich, nachdem er das dortige Gymnasium absolvirt, dem Studium der Philologie bestimmt. Gewisse Rücksichten nöthigten ihn später, diesem Lieblingsfach zu entsagen und sich der Jurisprudenz zuzuwenden, während deren Betreibung er jedoch schon damals, wie im Vorgefühl seiner künftigen Bestimmung, nicht nur die lebenden fremden Sprachen, sondern auch Sanscrit, Hebräisch und Arabisch mit vieler Liebe studierte.

> \*) Dem Inhalte des Buches und, wie ich mich recht zu erinnern glaube, auch der ursprünglichen Absicht seines Verfassers gemäss sollte demselben eine ausführliche Abhandlung über die Galla, historisch, geographisch und ethnographisch beigegeben werden; allein diese wurde leider nur begonnen, und was ich in dieser Beziehung geben kann sind nur Andeutungen; denn nicht ursprüngliche Wahl sondern traurige Nothwendigkeit hat mich auf die von meinem Bruder betretene Bahn geführt und mir die Pflicht auferlegt, das von ihm Geförderte so viel möglich der Wissenschaft zinsbar zu machen. Ich glaube jedoch die Hoffnung aussprechen zu dürfen, dass ich, wenn sich günstige Umstände vereinigen, in entschiedenerer und ausschliesslicherer Weise in die Fussstapfen meines Bruders eintreten werde, als diess bis jetzt der Fall sein konnte, und dass besonders dann, wenn ich nach gehörigen Vorstudien mit einem noch lebenden Galla-Referenten meines Bruders in Vernehmen treten kann, manches Unvollendete vollendet und so für die Wissenschaft gerettet wird, was für jetzt als unbearbeiteter Stoff nutzlos und brach vor mir liegt.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 42 (Book Page XXXV) --&gt;
*(— XXXV —)*

Als er im Jahre 1837 nach München gekommen war, um seine akademische Laufbahn als Jurist zu beendigen, wurde er zum Lehrer des Prinzen Louis in Bayern berufen und nach der Rückkehr Sr. Hoheit des Herzogs Maximilian in Bayern von seiner Reise in den Orient mit der Erziehung der von demselben aus der Sclaverei losgekauften und mit nach München gebrachten vier Schwarzen betraut. Er übernahm das seltene Amt voll Besorgniss für die neue Störung seiner Studien auf der einen, und wegen eines aus vielen Gründen möglichen Misslingens des Unternehmens auf der anderen Seite. Doch zog ihn die Neuheit der Sache an, und die wissenschaftliche Neugierde, ob er nicht Interessantes über die Heimath und früheren Verhältnisse der Fremden erfahren und so vielleicht etwas zur näheren Kenntniss von Afrika beitragen könne, liess ihn muthig das Werk am 18. Nov. 1838 beginnen.

Es ist leicht begreiflich, welche Schwierigkeiten der erste Anfang eines Unterrichtes gehabt habe, wo das dem Lehrer sonst zur Einwirkung auf die Schüler vor allem nöthige Mittel des gegenseitigen Verständnisses abging, um so mehr, wenn man bedenkt, dass den ersten dessfallsigen Bemühungen fast unübersteigliche Hindernisse in dem bekannten allen Africanern theils angebornen theils durch die Verhältnisse aufgedrungenen Misstrauen gegen Weisse entgegentraten; dazu kam noch, dass die Knaben nicht begreifen konnten, was man mit ihnen vorhabe, und so gingen denn mehrere mühselige Monate hin, während welcher mein Bruder täglich viele Stunden verhöhnt und verlacht, buchstäblich mit Händen und Füssen docirte.

Endlich gegen Ende Januar 1839 gewann er durch einen glücklich benützten Umstand — durch liebevolle Pflege eines seiner Zöglinge in einer unbedeutenden Krankheit — den ersten und wichtigsten Sieg, das Vertrauen desselben und wie bei ihm, so sah er bald auch bei den übrigen den hartnäckigen Argwohn in Zutraulichkeit und innige kindliche Neigung übergehen. Von diesem Augenblicke datiren sich die ersten fragmentarischen Nachrichten aus dem Leben eines jeden Einzelnen und es zeigte
*(Sig. 3\*)*

&lt;!-- PDF Page 43 (Book Page XXXVI) --&gt;
*(— XXXVI —)*

sich vor Allem, dass, während der Herzog ihm drei Nubier und einen Abyssinier zur Erziehung übergeben zu haben meinte, er statt dessen die Repräsentanten von vier verschiedenen Völkern, einen *Galla*, einen *Umale*, einen *Darfurianer* und einen *Denka* vor sich hatte, die aus ihrer Heimath gestohlen und durch den Sclavenhandel an den Nil verkauft, nur durch das Bedürfniss gegenseitiger Verständigung gezwungen waren, sehr nothdürftig den arabischen Vulgärdialact radebrechen zu lernen, selbst aber vier von einander ganz verschiedene, weder unter sich noch mit den bekannten africanischen, namentlich mit den semitischen verwandte Sprachen als Muttersprachen reden; ferner, dass drei von ihnen bei der Erwerbung durch den Herzog nicht länger als ein Jahr von ihrer Heimath entfernt waren und bei einem hinreichenden Alter und vortrefflichen Anlagen nicht nur linguistische Mittheilungen, sondern auch Urtheile über die Verhältnisse ihrer Heimath in Aussicht stellten, die den Berichten unserer bisherigen Reisenden in diesen Ländern an die Seite treten, ja aus mehreren Gründen sogar sicherere Aufschlüsse geben konnten.

Die ersten immerhin noch mit sehr grossen Schwierigkeiten erlangten Resultate der nun begonnenen Forschungen beschränkten sich auf Bestätigung der in den Reisebeschreibungen von Ludolf, Brown, Salt, Seetzen, Burckhart, Rüppell und Bruce enthaltenen Angaben, Bereicherung der dort verzeichneten Vocabularien und in der Fixirung verwandtschaftlicher Verhältnisse der einzelnen Referenten; auch fallen schon in jene Zeit die ersten Kartenentwürfe und Mittheilungen interessanter Gebete und Lieder, wie sie das Gedächtniss und der jedoch nicht ohne Rückfälle in das alte Misstrauen und in tagelange Verschlossenheit gebliebene gute Wille wiedergeben mochten. Es gehörte grosse Vorsicht dazu, hier sicher zu gehen, denn die geringste Veranlassung brachte bisweilen die Versicherung zu Wege, „dass Alles bisher Zugestandene nur erdichtet sei und „dass sie eigentlich aus ihrem Lande gar nichts wüssten; weil „sie als ganz kleine Kinder weggekommen seien“. Solche Rück-

&lt;!-- PDF Page 44 (Book Page XXXVII) --&gt;
*(— XXXVII —)*

fälle wurden jedoch immer seltener und konnten, nachdem Vergleichungen und Wiederholungen die früheren Angaben vorsichtig als richtig geprüft hatten, nicht mehr von langer Dauer sein, da zudem die Ueberzeugung, dass man es nur gut mit ihnen meine, als natürliche Folge der genaueren Bekanntschaft mit unseren Lebensverhältnissen und ihrer Fortschritte in den ihre Wissbegierden fesselnden Unterrichtsgegenständen täglich mehr Fuss fassen musste.

Der bisher eingeschlagene Weg von Vocabularien-Sammlung wurde jedoch bald verlassen, und zwar besonders in Folge der Aufmunterung und der Rathschläge, die mein Bruder von Prof. Karl Ritter in Berlin erhielt. Mein Bruder hatte nemlich diesem in africanischen Dingen mit Recht als erste Authorität verehrten Gelehrten über den Gang seiner Studien geschrieben und hatte die Freude, als Beweis der Theilnahme, welche er bei ihm erregt, einen Vortrag zugeschickt zu erhalten, welchen Herr Prof. Ritter in der Monatssitzung der geographischen Gesellschaft vom Mai 1839 über jene magere Mittheilung gehalten hatte. Von dieser Zeit an richtete mein Bruder alle seine Kraft darauf, mit seinen Zöglingen, wenn auch noch innerhalb eines beschränkten Horizonts, immerwährende Unterhaltung über alle Verhältnisse ihrer Länder zu pflegen; und während dieses seiner von Tag zu Tag gesteigerten Begeisterung gelang, suchte er das ohnehin vorhandene Heimweh so zu nähren und zu veredeln, dass die Ueberzeugung in ihnen recht lebhaft wurde, treue Mittheilungen an ihn, verbunden mit dem, was sie aus seiner Hand empfangen, seien die sicherste Gewähr, nicht nur ihre Sehnsucht nach dem Vaterlande einst stillen zu können, sondern auch für die Erreichung des weit edleren Zieles — aus der Fremde, in die sie ein hartes Geschick gestossen, zum Heil und Segen ihres Vaterlandes zu wirken und so den Beruf zu erfüllen, den ihnen eine höhere Hand auferlegt.

Der in diesem Sinne von nun an befolgten Methode; sich zusammenhängende vorher reiflich durchdachte Darstellungen über verlangte oder angebotene Themata dictiren zu lassen, ver-

&lt;!-- PDF Page 45 (Book Page XXXVIII) --&gt;
*(— XXXVIII —)*

dankte mein Bruder jene zahlreichen, höchst interessanten Aufsätze, welche ihm später neben der persönlichen Unterhaltung mit den Referenten selbst als Quellen und Fundgruben seiner linguistischen, geographischen und ethnographischen Studien dienten; und, da sie theilweise mit wahrer Gediegenheit und Rundung sowohl in Bezug auf Styl als auf Inhalt und Anordnung desselben abgefasst sind, eine Art Literatur der entsprechenden Völker zu repräsentiren Anspruch machen dürfen. Wie diess bei der Jugend der Referenten möglich sei, erweisst sich aus der Betrachtung, dass die Erzählungsgabe überhaupt ein Prädicat aller orientalischen Völker ist, und dass besonders jene Kinder der Natur von den frühesten Lebensjahren an mit allen Verhältnissen ihrer Eltern, Dorfbewohner und Volksstämme bis aufs kleinste vertraut werden und desshalb bei der uncivilisirten Völkern durchweg eigenthümlichen Schärfe der Beobachtung schon sehr frühzeitig zu einer klaren Anschauungsweise und Beurtheilung derselben gelangen.

Wie sich nun die auf solche Weise begonnene Ausbeute von Tag zu Tag mehrte, trat das Bedürfniss auf, der Bearbeitung des Einen oder Anderen eine ausschliesslichere Thätigkeit zuzuwenden. Die Heimath und Sprache des *Galla*, Akafed'e Dalle aus *Botchi* in *Hambo*, einer Provinz von *Liban*, (Stamm der *Boranna Galla*) der leider sein Vaterland nicht wiedersehen sollte, sondern seit dem 17. Mai 1841 in deutscher Erde schläft, lenkte zuerst meines Bruders Aufmerksamkeit auf sich. Jenes Volk, das in zahlreiche Stämme getheilt, seit der Mitte des 16. Jahrhunderts durch seine unaufhörlichen Einfälle und sein Drängen gegen Norden sich den Abyssiniern so furchtbar und geschichtlich merkwürdig gemacht hat, war durch den talentvollen Akafed'e auf das Trefflichste repräsentirt, und mein Bruder war, abgesehen von den politischen Rücksichten, die hier ein bedeutendes Gewicht in die Wagschale legten, vorzüglich auch durch die Einfachheit, den Wohlklang und die nach kurzen Studien schon klar erkannte grammatische Durchbildung der Galla-Sprache aufs Entschiedenste angezogen. Er wandte daher,

&lt;!-- PDF Page 46 (Book Page XXXIX) --&gt;
*(— XXXIX —)*

ohne dabei die anderen zu vernachlässigen, sein Hauptaugenmerk auf sie und gelangte in der kurzen Zeit von anderthalb Jahren soweit, dass er sie ziemlich geläufig sprach und freudig einer ersten Einladung der königl. Akademie der Wissenschaften in München zu Mittheilungen über die Resultate seiner Studien folgen konnte. In der Sitzung der philosophischen Klasse vom 2. Januar 1841 las er eine Darstellung über Beginn und Fortgang seiner Forschungen und einen Abriss der Grammatik der Galla-Sprache; zugleich legte er der Klasse ein ziemlich vollständiges Lexicon derselben und mehrere sprachlich und sachlich commentirte Dictate, Gebete, Lieder etc. vor. Eine ihm kurz vorher zugekommene Abhandlung über die *Galla* von *Limu* (Extrait du bulletin de la société géographique, Aout 1839), welche Herr Bibliothekar Jomard in der Akademie zu Paris gelesen hatte, war der erste schriftliche Beweis für die Richtigkeit seiner eigenen Resultate, denn er fand darin nur bekannte Sachen, sowohl im geographischen Theile als auch in den wenigen dort mitgetheilten Sprachproben, wo namentlich die Gebete dieselben sind, wie er sie von seinem Akafed'e niedergeschrieben, nur mit einer andern Orthographie und einer durch sie bedingten verschiedenen Uebersetzung. Was dort über Sitten und Gebräuche gesagt ist, stimmt mit wenigen Ausnahmen mit seinen Wahrnehmungen überein. Viel wichtiger als diess, war aber der sprechende Beweiss, den ihm um dieselbe Zeit das Glück in der Person eines zweiten Galla zuführte. Osthu Aga aus *Urgeza* in *Sibu* wurde auf seiner Durchreise durch München von seinem Befreier aus der Sclaverei, Mr. Pell meinem Bruder zwei Monate lang zur Verfügung gestellt. Durch ihn wurde nicht nur Alles bisher Gewonnene und Erlernte als authentisch bestätigt, sondern auch Stoff zu dialectischen Untersuchungen gegeben und die Zahl der Dictate, Gebete und Lieder, der geographischen Data etc. um manches Interessante vermehrt. Zwischen Aga und Akafed'e, welche schon bald schmerzlich schwelgend in der Erinnerung an ihr verlorenes Vaterland in inniger Freundschaft sich aneinander geschmiegt hatten, veranlasste mein Bru-

&lt;!-- PDF Page 47 (Book Page XL) --&gt;
*(— XL —)*

der eine Galla-Correspondenz, welche in 14 Briefen bestehend neben ihrem linguistischen Werthe einen schönen Massstab für den Grad geistiger und gemüthlicher Anlagen beider Briefsteller abgeben. Durch Aga lernte mein Bruder auch die vermeintliche Abyssinierin des Fürsten Pückler Muscau, *Bilile* (gest. am 27. Oct. 1840) kennen, welche aus dem Galla-Lande *Guma* war und Aga mehrere ihrer Lieder gelehrt hatte; auch sie sind der Zahl der von meinem Bruder verzeichneten beigefügt worden. Ein dritter Galla endlich, Aman Gonda aus *Hibi* in *Goma*, von Sr. Hoheit dem Herzog Paul in Württemberg nach Europa gebracht, welchen mein Bruder auf die freundliche Einladung dieses wissenschaftlichen Fürsten zweimal auf längere Zeit besuchte, schloss die Reihe der Referenten. Mein Bruder legte auf die Mittheilungen Amans, obwohl er der jüngste von allen dreien ist, sehr grossen Werth, weil er als der Sohn eines *aba fuño* (wörtl. Strickmeister, Gerichtsperson im Dienste der *zoreza*, Fürsten) eine bessere Erziehung genossen, über die Verhältnisse seines Vaterlandes desshalb genauere Kenntnisse mitgebracht hatte, und auch seine Sprache orthographischer spricht als die beiden anderen.

Da diese 3 Referenten in einer gewissen Reihenfolge vor dem Forum der Forschungen meines Bruders auftraten, so musste die Vergleichung sowohl als das jederzeit Neuhinzugekommene bedeutende Veränderungen und Bereicherung des Früheren bedingen, wodurch sich denn nach und nach aus dem der Akademie vorgelegten Lexicon, wo z. B. die Orthographie, auch die Etymologie noch auf schwachen Füssen stand, gegenwärtiges nach Inhalt und Form auf festen Grundsätzen gebautes und mit den in der Grammatik aufgestellten Principien in Einklang gebrachtes Wörterbuch hervorbildete. \*)

> \*) Den zweiten Theil desselben: Englisch-Galla, und falls es für erspriesslich erachtet werden sollte, auch den dritten Theil: Deutsch-Galla, werde ich, sobald es mir möglich ist, diesem ersten folgen lassen. Die genannte Grammatik hat leider nicht in allen Theilen

&lt;!-- PDF Page 48 (Book Page XLI) --&gt;
*(— XLI —)*

Neben dieser mehr ausschliesslichen Beschäftigung mit seinen Galla-Referenten ging die Erforschung der Sprache und heimathlichen Verhältnisse eines zweiten seiner Zöglinge einher, der sein Interesse in gleichem wo nicht höherem Grade in Anspruch zu nehmen bestimmt war. Es ist diess Djalo Djondan Aré aus dem Volke der *Yumale*, welche unterhalb *Kordofan* unter dem 12° und 13° N. B. und dem 46° und 47° O. L. unter der Oberherrlichkeit des Königs von *Takle* (*Takele*, *Tokole*) leben. Er ist zu *Deling'n* in *Tumale-Tokoken* geboren und der Neffe des noch bei seinem Weggang wenigstens dort regierenden erblichen Fürsten (Königs) *Wofter Mat*. Seine Sprache stellt in Bezug auf Laut, Charakter und innern Bau fast das directe Gegentheil der Galla-Sprache dar, denn während jene weich und melodisch klingt, nicht unähnlich der Italienischen und gleich dieser bei einem entschiedenen Vorwiegen der Vocale verhältnissmässig eine grosse Einfachheit in den Formen und in der Ausdrucksweise zeigt, klingt die Sprache des *Umale* hart, so zu sagen holpericht und bietet durch eine wahrhafte Ueberfüllung an Consonanten selbst unserem deutschen an Härten gewöhnten Organe Anfangs sehr grosse Schwierigkeiten im Nachsprechen dar; auch sind die Formen so ausserordentlich mannichfaltig und eigenthümlich und die Zahl der Worte und Begriffe in dieser Sprache

> *(Footnote continued from p. XL:)* jene Ausarbeitung erhalten, wie diess in den Zeit-, Zahl- und Fürwörtern der Fall ist, auch sind die Sprachproben, welche mein Bruder als Anhang der Grammatik beigeben wollte, nicht vorbereitet für diesen Zweck, was sehr zu bedauern ist, zumal in Bezug auf die Syntax der Galla-Sprache, über welche mein Bruder so viele Materialien gesammelt hatte und deren philologischen Reiz er mir immer als so gross und hinreissend schilderte. Ich werde aber gleichwohl die Grammatik in der unvollständigen Form, wie sie mein Bruder hinterlassen, der Oeffentlichkeit übergeben und im Stande sein, ihr ausser den Kartenentwürfen wenigstens einige Sprachproben mit Nutzen beizufügen.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 49 (Book Page XLII) --&gt;
*(— XLII —)*

übersteigt jene des Galla in einem solchen Grade, dass mein Bruder, wenn er länger gelebt hätte, erst spät und schwer so tief in sie eingedrungen wäre, wie ihm dies in relativ so kurzer Zeit mit dem Galla gelungen ist. Gerade aber dieser Reichthum der Sprache und mehr noch der hohe Grad von Bildung der *Yumale*, (wenn ich mich dieses Ausdrucks von einem centralafricanischen Volke bedienen darf) auf welchen die Mittheilungen des mit den schönsten Anlagen begabten Djalo schliessen lassen, ermuthigten meinen Bruder, hier Gleiches zu leisten; denn so gross immer die Schwierigkeiten sind, welche sich der Erlernung und gänzlichen Erfassung dieser Sprache entgegenstellen, so lohnend wird dagegen die lexicalische und grammatikalische Bearbeitung derselben für die Sprachforschung im Allgemeinen sein und so gross sind auch die Schätze, welche durch die Kenntniss derselben erschlossen werden können. Von Djalo's Dictaten nemlich \*) gilt besonders, was oben von deren Gediegenheit nach Form und Inhalt gesagt wurde; und es muss hier dem Obigen noch beigefügt werden, dass denselben überall das Gepräge des gereiften Urtheils und der kritischen Wahrheit aufgedrückt ist, eine Vereinigung von Vorzügen, die sie gewiss einst, wenn sie gesichtet und commentirt der Oeffentlichkeit übergeben werden können, als wesentliche Bereicherung der Wissenschaft erscheinen lassen wird. Aus dieser gewiss nicht übertriebenen Schilderung geht hervor, dass die Begeisterung für diese interessanten Studien als ein Erbtheil von meinem Bruder auf mich übergegangen ist, und ich nehme hier Gelegenheit zu erwähnen, dass

> \*) Ich habe drei Bände davon in Händen, in welchen alle nur denklichen Verhältnisse jenes interessanten Volkes, theils fragmentarisch, theils in grösserer Ausführlichkeit abgehandelt sind. Die Mittheilungen scheiden sich in solche, die Djalo als Reproductionen aus seinem eminenten Gedächtnisse gegeben und in solche, die als freie Aufsätze, als eigene Producte des Referenten sich darstellen.

&lt;!-- PDF Page 50 (Book Page XLIII) --&gt;
*(— XLIII —)*

ich fest entschlossen bin, so weit es meinen schwachen Kräften gelingen mag, auch hier den von meinem Bruder eingeschlagenen Weg zu verfolgen, und theilweise wenigstens abzuzahlen, was er der Wissenschaft schuldig bleiben musste. Der Anfang dazu ist gemacht, denn Djalo lebt in München und unterstützt von seinem eigenen regen Streben, mitzuwirken an dem schönen Werke, bin ich bereits durch tägliche Conferenzen mit ihm in den Vorhof des zu erforschenden grossen Gebäudes eingeführt; ob ich zu einem befriedigenden Ziele gelange, wird die nächste Zukunft lehren.

Der dritte Zögling meines Bruders ist Mussalam Motekutu aus *Methem* in *Darfur* etwa 7 Tagreisen südlich von *Kobbe*, der Hauptstadt des Landes. Auch seine Mittheilungen sind von nicht unbeträchtlichem Werthe und seine Dictate verdienen eine Bearbeitung recht wohl; denn wenn dieselben auch in Styl und Inhalt mehr den Ausdruck der Kindlichkeit an sich tragen, und die Erinnerungen Mussalams überhaupt nicht so umfassend zu sein scheinen, wie jene der bis jetzt genannten Referenten meines Bruders, so wird doch immerhin die Wiederaufnahme der Forschungen über seine heimathlichen Verhältnisse und seine Sprache jetzt, wo er reifer geworden, ausser zahlreichen ethnographischen und geographischen Notizen auch die Herstellung eines sehr reichen Wörterbuchs und eines Abrisses der Grammatik seiner Sprache möglich machen. Mussalam spricht sie jetzt noch geläufig, und es bietet dieselbe, soweit mir meine geringe Einsicht in ihren Bau und Charakter ein Urtheil hierüber erlaubt, der Erlernung bei Weitem nicht jene Schwierigkeiten dar, wie die des *Umale*.

Ueber den vierten Zögling endlich, Awan, will ich meinen Bruder selbst einige Worte reden lassen. In der oben erwähnten Vorlesung in der Akademie sagte er unter Anderm: „bei diesem Knaben, dem jüngsten von Allen, er mag etwa 12 Jahre alt sein, habe ich eine psychologisch merkwürdige Erfahrung gemacht, die wohl werth ist, zum Gegenstand besonderen Nachdenkens erhoben zu werden. Während des ganzen Jahres

&lt;!-- PDF Page 51 (Book Page XLIV) --&gt;
*(— XLIV —)*

nemlich, wo ich mit den drei übrigen so froh vorwärts arbeitete, antwortete mir dieser immer traurig, er wisse nichts mehr von seiner Heimath, er sei zu klein gewesen, als er fortgekommen. Im Anfange glaubte ich, das alte Misstrauen spräche aus ihm; weil er aber in allem Uebrigen aufrichtig war, so nahm ich später seine Aussage für wahr und liess ihn, da die anderen Quellen um so reichlicher flossen, lange Zeit unbeachtet. Diese unwillkührliche Vernachlässigung von meiner Seite, verbunden mit dem unter Knaben so gewöhnlich vorkommenden Muthwillen gegen Schwächere, hätte aber fast schlimme Folgen gehabt. Der Knabe wurde still, mürrisch — und endlich fand ich ihn viele Tage nacheinander weinend, ohne dass ich im Stande gewesen wäre, den Grund aus ihm herauszubringen. Unter vier Augen aber sagte er mir, dass er Heimweh habe und sich nach seinen Verwandten sehne; auch seien ihm die Namen seines Vaters, seiner Mutter, seines Bruders und sein eigener eingefallen, aber „ich würde ihm nicht glauben“. Ich versicherte nun mit innerer Reue über mein bisheriges Verfahren den Weinenden meines vollkommenen Zutrauens; dann drang ich noch weiter in ihn, ob er gar kein Wort aus seiner Sprache behalten habe, und wirklich fielen ihm nach langem Nachdenken zwei ein; *kur* der Berg und *kir* der Fluss. Zu meiner grossen Freude fand ich die beiden Wörter in Rüppels Vocabularien unter der Aufschrift *Dar Denka*. Nachdem ihm durch ununterbrochenes Nachdenken und stetes Zureden von meiner Seite in den folgenden Tagen noch 6 bis 8 mit dem Rüppel'schen Vocabular übereinstimmende Wörter eingefallen waren, nannte ich ihm den Volksnamen *Denkawi*, den er mit Freuden sogleich für den seines Volkes erkannte. Es war hierauf herrlich; das mehrerwähnte Vocabular mit ihm durchzugehen und die Wirkung der alten bekannten Laute auf den Knaben zu beobachten, rührend auch die Freude seiner drei schwarzen Freunde, welche ich eine Viertelstunde darauf, nachdem ihnen die Neuigkeit bekannt geworden war, weinend an seinem Halse antraf, voll Freude, dass nun auch er ihnen

&lt;!-- PDF Page 52 (Book Page XLV) --&gt;
*(— XLV —)*

gleichsam ebenbürtig geworden durch das Wiederauffinden seines Vaterlandes. Von jenem Augenblick an begann auch bei ihm ein ganz anderes Leben. Durch angestrengtes Nachdenken über seine Vergangenheit kam er in den Stand, ein jetzt bereits gegen 500 Wörter enthaltendes Vocabular zusammenzuschreiben, und ich habe die Hoffnung noch nicht aufgegeben, dass ihm endlich auch noch Zusammenhängendes zurückkommen wird.“ Letztere Hoffnung wurde nicht erfüllt, denn die Ausbeute blieb bei Awan auf Vocabularienvergrösserung beschränkt.

Man sieht aus dem Vorhergehenden, dass die Aufgabe, welche sich mein Bruder gestellt, eine grosse und vielgegliederte war und dass die Anerkennung, welche der theilweisen Lösung derselben und seinem Streben überhaupt von Seite der Münchener gelehrten Welt wie von auswärtigen Gelehrten zu Theil wurde, und stets neuermuthigend auf ihn wirkte, eine wohlverdiente war. Am wohlthuendsten und förderlichsten aber für das Gedeihen seiner Arbeiten, besonders zu der Zeit, wo sich das Verhältniss zu seinen Schülern gelöst hatte, war die rege Theilnahme, welche die geographische Gesellschaft und die African civilisation society in London in wiederholten Schreiben ausdrückten und die durch die hilfreiche Hand des Vicepräsidenten letzterer Gesellschaft des Sir Thomas Dyke Acland auch materiell fördernd auftrat. Herr Acland liess nemlich meinen Bruder nicht nur alle in der letzten Zeit erschienenen auf seine Arbeiten bezüglichen Bücher zustellen, sondern sandte auch freiwillig Unterstützungen, denen allein mein Bruder es damals zu danken hatte, dass seine Studien nicht eine höchst nachtheilige Störung und Unterbrechung betraf. Ich muss diess um so mehr hier öffentlich dankend anerkennen, als Herr Acland diese Opfer rein aus wissenschaftlichem Interesse für die Sache brachte. Diese Theilnahme und die damit verbundene Aussicht auf eine einstmalige Verwendung für eine von England ausgehende wissenschaftliche Mission oder Expedition nach Africa, endlich das Interesse, welches ein deutscher Fürst an diesen

&lt;!-- PDF Page 53 (Book Page XLVI) --&gt;
*(— XLVI —)*

Studien nahm, hielt lange Zeit seine wankend gewordene Gesundheit aufrecht. Als nemlich gegenwärtiges Lexicon beendet war, wurde es Sr. Königl.
